II. 
PURPOSE OF TESTIMONY 
22 
Q. 
What is the purpose of your testimony in this proceeding? 
23 


--- Page 6 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
4 
 
A. 
Our testimony primarily covers the following topics:  
1 
• An overview of the factors driving the proposed electric and gas 
2 
revenue requirements for the twelve months ending December 31, 2022 
3 
(“Rate Year”); 
4 
• The steps taken by the Company to mitigate customer bill impacts in 
5 
light of the COVID-19 pandemic and current economic conditions; 
6 
• Historic financial statements and statistical data as required by the 
7 
Commission; 
8 
• Rate base for the twelve months ended September 30, 2020 (“Historic 
9 
Year”) through the Rate Year; 
10 
• A comparison of the projected revenues, expenses and rate base for the 
11 
Rate Year to the Historic Year; 
12 
• The development of the Rate Year electric and gas revenue 
13 
requirements; 
14 
• The Company’s requests related to certain deferral accounting and 
15 
reconciliation mechanisms; and 
16 
• The Company’s forecasted financial information for the two annual 
17 
periods beyond the Rate Year to provide a basis for settlement 
18 
discussions regarding a multi-year rate plan. 
19 
Q. 
Please describe your testimony and how it is organized. 
20 
A. 
The Accounting Panel testimony covers the below-listed topics and exhibits.  
21 
All of these exhibits were prepared under our supervision and direction, but 
22 
rely on input from other Company witnesses.  Certain projections will be

III. 
RATE RELIEF REQUEST  
15 
Q. 
What amount of rate relief is the Company requesting? 
16 
A. 
For electric, the Company is requesting approximately $24.5 million of rate 
17 
relief for the Rate Year.  That amount equates to approximately a 3.3% overall 
18 
increase in customer bills and approximately a 5.8% increase on a delivery bill 
19 


--- Page 8 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
6 
 
basis. 
1 
 
For gas, the Company is requesting approximately $9.8 million of rate relief 
2 
for the Rate Year.  That amount equates to approximately a 4.0% overall 
3 
increase in customer bills and approximately a 6.9% increase on a delivery bill 
4 
basis. 
5 
Q. 
In developing the rate relief request, what steps has the Company taken to 
6 
moderate customer bill impacts in light of the COVID-19 pandemic and 
7 
current economic conditions? 
8 
A. 
The Company is making a number of proposals, noted briefly here and 
9 
described in further detail later within this testimony, as part of the rate relief 
10 
request that have the effect of moderating customer bill impacts.  For one, the 
11 
Company is continuing its Business Cost Optimization (“BCO”) program and 
12 
reflects all of the achieved-to-date and projected savings from the program as 
13 
reductions to the revenue requirement.  Additionally, the Company proposes to 
14 
shorten the remaining amortization period associated with unprotected excess 
15 
deferred income taxes from 12 years to 3 years and extend the remaining 
16 
recovery period for deferred storm costs from 3 years to 5 years.  The 
17 
Company is also proposing to recover energy efficiency costs over 10 years, as 
18 
discussed in the direct testimony of the Company’s Customer Service Panel. 
19 
In order to mitigate the proposed rate increase in RY1 relative to RY2 and 
20 
RY3, the Company is delaying proposed reductions to gas depreciation lives to 
21 
RY2 and delaying the onset of recovery of the depreciation reserve deficiency 
22 
until RY3.  Additionally, the Company is levelizing pension and Other Post-
23 


--- Page 9 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
7 
 
Employment Benefits (“OPEB”) costs over the three RYs as such costs would 
1 
otherwise have been higher in RY1. 
2 
Q. 
What are the specific drivers of the requested rate increases? 
3 
A.

4 
driving the need for increased electric and gas base rate revenues:

Q. 
Please discuss the “new infrastructure investment, including return, 
6 
depreciation and property taxes” item shown in the above table. 
7 
A. 
One of the primary drivers of the requested rate increases is the continued need 
8 
to upgrade, reinforce, rebuild and invest in the Company’s infrastructure.  The 
9 
carrying cost of this new investment (i.e., cost of capital and depreciation at 
10 
current rates), offset by a decrease in property taxes in the Rate Year, is $13 
11 
million for electric and $7 million for gas.  The Electric Infrastructure and 
12 
Operations Panel (“EIOP”), the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
13 
(“GIOP”), the Customer Service Panel, the Information Technology (“IT”) 
14 


--- Page 10 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
8 
 
Panel and the Environmental Health and Safety (“EH&S”) Panel explain these 
1 
needs in greater detail. 
2 
Q. 
What impact does the return on equity (“ROE”) and projected interest cost 
3 
have in this rate request? 
4 
A. 
Under the Commission’s rate order in the Company’s most recent electric and 
5 
gas base rate proceedings (Case 18-E-0067, Case 18-G-0068) (“2019 Rate 
6 
Order”), current electric and gas rates reflect an overall rate of return of 6.96%, 
7 
including an ROE of 9.0%.  The weighted cost of long-term debt included is 
8 
5.14%.  As shown in Exhibit AP-5 and discussed in the direct testimony of 
9 
Company witness Saegusa, the electric and gas revenue requirements in this 
10 
case reflect an overall rate of return of 7.04%, based on a 9.5% ROE and a 
11 
weighted cost of long-term debt of 4.62%.  Although Company witness 
12 
Villadsen provided in her direct testimony that an ROE estimate of 9.75% is 
13 
appropriate for the Company, the Company’s electric and gas revenue 
14 
requirements reflect a 9.5% ROE.  The Company selected the lower ROE in 
15 
order to mitigate customer bill impacts and facilitate reaching a multi-year rate 
16 
plan through settlement.  As further justified by Company witness Saegusa, the 
17 
Company selected an equity ratio of 50%.  Should the Commission assign 
18 
greater risks to the Company, the Company does not waive its right to a higher 
19 
return corresponding to such greater risks.  Approximately $2 million of the 
20 
electric revenue requirement increase and $1 million of the gas revenue 
21 
requirement increase are attributable to the higher financing costs, including 
22 
the cost of capital associated with growth in rate base.   
23 


--- Page 11 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
9 
 
Q. 
Please discuss the next item in the table, “depreciation changes due to 
1 
proposed rates.” 
2 
A. 
As discussed in the direct testimony of the Depreciation Panel, the Company is 
3 
proposing to change its depreciation rates.  These changes account for $2 
4 
million of the electric rate increase.  There is no impact on the gas rate increase 
5 
in the Rate Year, as the Company has elected to defer the change in 
6 
depreciation rates until RY2, as discussed below. 
7 
Q. 
What effects do projected sales revenues have on the proposed revenue 
8 
requirements? 
9 
A. 
As discussed in the direct testimony of the Electric and Gas Forecasting 
10 
Panels, net sales revenues are projected to decrease by $9 million and $3 
11 
million for electric and gas, respectively, and the revenue requirements are 
12 
reflective of these changes in sales. 
13 
Q. 
Are operation and maintenance (“O&M”) expenses contributing to the need for 
14 
the electric and gas rate increases? 
15 
A. 
No, the Company is seeking electric and gas O&M expense levels that are 
16 
lower than the levels reflected in current rates.  The net decrease in O&M 
17 
levels is primarily driven by the Company’s continuing initiatives to reduce 
18 
costs and work more efficiently through our BCO program, lower pension, and 
19 
OPEB costs, and our request to defer energy efficiency costs and recover these 
20 
costs over a 10-year period. 
21 
Q. 
Are regulatory amortizations contributing to the need for the electric and gas 
22 
rate increases? 
23 


--- Page 12 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
10 
 
A. 
Expiring customer credits and recovery of additional deferred costs necessitate 
1 
an $8 million increase in electric rates, but do not materially impact gas rates.  
2 
Q. 
Please discuss the final item in the table, “income taxes.” 
3 
A. 
The Company substantially mitigated its rate request by accelerating the pass 
4 
back of certain excess deferred income taxes resulting from the Tax Cuts and 
5 
Jobs Act of 2017 (the “2017 Tax Act”).  In the 2019 Rate Order, the Company 
6 
was authorized to amortize unprotected excess deferred income taxes over 15 
7 
years.  In this proceeding, the Company is proposing to refund the remaining 
8 
excess deferred income taxes resulting from the tax rate reductions that are 
9 
related to unprotected property over three years (2022 – 2024), rather than the 
10 
remaining 12-year period (2022 -2033) approved in the 2019 Rate Order. The 
11 
acceleration of this refund lowers the electric and gas rate requests by $9 
12 
million and $3 million, respectively. This reduction was partially offset by a 
13 
similar reduction to three years for the recovery of the remaining excess 
14 
deferred income taxes related to non-property, which increases the electric and 
15 
gas rate requests by $3 million and $1 million, respectively. 
16 
Q. 
Do any of your exhibits address in further detail the elements of the revenue 
17 
requirement you have summarized? 
18 
A. 
Yes, Schedule 1 of the AP-3 Exhibits. 
19 
 
 
 
 
20

V. 
RATE BASE 
9 
Q. 
What exhibits support the Company’s electric and gas rate base amounts in this 
10 
filing?  
11 
A.  
The AP-2 Exhibits contain summaries and details of the Company’s rate base 
12 
for the Historic Year per books and the forecasted rate base for the Rate Year. 
13 
Q.  
Please describe the presentation of rate base in the AP-2 Exhibits.    
14 
A.  
The presentation approach is the same for both the electric and gas rate base 
15 
exhibits.  There are a total of six schedules in each exhibit.  Schedule 1 
16 
summarizes the overall rate base calculation for the Historic Year and Rate 
17 
Year.  Schedule 2 shows the details of the forecasted net plant and non-interest 
18 
bearing Construction Work in Process (“CWIP”) calculation, as shown on page 
19 
1, lines 1 to 11.  Schedule 3 provides the details of the working capital 
20 
components, unamortized premium & discount expense and customer advance 
21 
construction, as shown on page 1, lines 12 to 14.  Schedule 4 provides the 
22 
details of the current and projected deferred balance from reconciliation 
23 


--- Page 16 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
14 
 
mechanisms, as shown on page 1, line 15.  Schedule 5 shows the details of 
1 
accumulated deferred federal and state tax balances, as shown on schedule 1, 
2 
lines 17 to 18.  Schedule 6 provides a detailed calculation of the Earning Base

1 of the AP-2 exhibits.

21 
the AP-2 exhibits.  Working capital rate base contains three categories: 
22 
Materials and Supplies, Prepayments, and Cash Working Capital. 
23 


--- Page 18 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
16 
 
a. Material and Supplies 
1 
Q. 
How did you determine the average balance of Materials and Supplies rate 
2 
base on schedule 3 of the AP-2 exhibits? 
3 
A.  
The Company has taken the same approach used in past Company rate cases.  
4 
The Rate Year forecast of Materials and Supplies inventory generally 
5 
represents the Historic Year amount escalated using the general escalation 
6 
factor.  For gas, we excluded from the rate base inventory balances of gas 
7 
stored underground and Liquefied Natural Gas in storage. 
8 
b. Prepayments 
9 
Q. 
What is included in the “Prepayments” category of working capital rate base 
10 
on schedule 3 of the AP-2 exhibits? 
11 
A. 
The prepayments component of working capital rate base includes Local 
12 
Property Taxes, Remarket, Computer License, Insurance, NYPSC Assessment 
13 
and New York State Gross Receipts Tax. 
14 
Q. 
Please explain how you developed the Rate Year Rate base amount for the 
15 
Prepayments items? 
16 
A.  
We projected all prepayments except for the prepaid property taxes at the 
17 
Historic Year amount plus general inflation.  We forecasted prepaid property 
18 
taxes, the predominant prepayment item, to increase based on the projected 
19 
level of property tax bills.   
20 
c. Cash Working Capital 
21 
Q. 
Please explain the allowance for the cash working capital component of 
22 
working capital rate base on schedule 3 of the AP-2 exhibits. 
23 


--- Page 19 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
17 
 
A. 
We determined the cash working capital component of working capital rate 
1 
base following well-established Commission practice, which includes applying 
2 
the 1/8 FERC Working Capital Formula.  As such, we performed separate 
3 
calculations of the rate base amount for electric and gas.  For each, we started 
4 
with projected total O&M expense from Schedule 6 of the AP-3 exhibits and 
5 
eliminated the expenses listed below to arrive at the level of O&M expense 
6 
that would be subject to the 1/8 FERC Working Capital Formula. 
7 
For electric, we eliminated purchased power and fuel expense, uncollectible 
8 
reserve, low income, system benefit charge (“SBC”), and renewable portfolio 
9 
charges.  
10 
For gas, we eliminated purchased gas expense, uncollectible reserve, low 
11 
income, and SBC.  
12 
For electric, while we eliminated fuel and purchased power from the 1/8 FERC 
13 
Working Capital Formula, we made a separate working capital adjustment to 
14 
account for the time lag between when fuel costs are paid to the New York 
15 
Independent System Operator and other agencies on a weekly basis and when 
16 
payments are collected from customers.  This additional element of the cash 
17 
working capital allowance adds $8.5 million to the cash working capital rate 
18 
base for electric, as shown on schedule 3 of the AP-2 exhibits. 
19 
2. 
Unamortized Premium & Discount and Customer 
20 
Advance for Construction

1 
balance of debt discounts, premiums and expenses, as additions to rate base.  
2 
The Company applied the forecasted unamortized balances as of the Rate Year 
3 
for inclusion in the rate base calculation.   
4 
            Customer advance for construction represents the amount billed to customers 
5 
and others for the construction necessary to provide utility service to their 
6 
premises (rather than for general system service) and represent a reduction to 
7 
rate base.  For electric, the Company adjusted the Historic Year level for 
8 
deposits expected to be returned to customers to arrive at the Rate Year 
9 
level.  For gas, the Historic Year levels of these items were carried forward to 
10 
the Rate Year.

E. 
Detailed Development of Accumulated Deferred Income 
4 
Taxes  
5 
Q. 
How were Accumulated Deferred Federal Income Taxes on schedule 5 of the 
6 
AP-2 exhibits developed? 
7 
A. 
Accumulated Deferred Federal Income Taxes for plant-related items were 
8 
developed using data from the Company’s capital budget and tax depreciation 
9 
models.  The Company calculates the rate base impact for federal deferred 
10 
income taxes by using a proration methodology that is required by U.S. 
11 
Treasury Regulation §1.167(I)-1h(6)(ii).  The Internal Revenue Service 
12 
(“IRS”) has determined that any revenue requirement calculation that employs 
13 
a future test period is subject to the proration requirement.  Accordingly, in 
14 
calculating the deferred taxes associated with the Rate Year, a proration is 
15 
required that provides a weighted average to the monthly deferred tax activity 
16 
arising under the Company’s projections.  The Company applied this 
17 
methodology in this case to avoid non-compliance with IRS normalization 
18 
rules.  
19 
Q. 
How were Accumulated Deferred State Income Taxes on schedule 5 of the 
20 
AP-2 exhibits developed? 
21 
A. 
Accumulated Deferred State Income Taxes for plant-related items were 
22 
developed using data from the Company’s capital budget and tax depreciation 
23 


--- Page 22 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
20 
 
models.  The forecasted rate year balance is based on 50% of beginning and 
1 
50% of ending forecasted balance. 
2 
Q. 
How were Deferred Investment Tax Credits on schedule 5 of the AP-2 exhibits 
3 
developed? 
4 
A. 
Deferred Investment Tax Credits are amortized over the average service lives 
5 
of the property that generated the tax credits.  The forecasted rate year balance 
6 
is based on the historical year balance plus the future forecasted amortization. 
7 
F. 
Rate Base Over/Under Capital Adjustment  
8 
Q. 
Please explain rate base over/under capitalization adjustment on AP-2 
9 
Exhibits, schedule 6. 
10 
A. 
Rate base over/under capitalization adjustment on AP-2 Exhibits, schedule 6, 
11 
reflects the required adjustment to rate base to make earnings base equal to 
12 
capitalization.  This Earnings Base Capitalization (“EB/Cap”) Adjustment has 
13 
been required by the Commission in past proceedings to synchronize rate base 
14 
plus interest bearing items (together, “Earnings Base”) with the total 
15 
capitalization employed in utility service.  Line 42 on AP-2 Exhibit, schedule 
16 
6, shows the EB/Cap adjustment amount to each electric and gas rate base.  
17 
The EB/Cap adjustment amount is calculated by taking the total capitalization 
18 
amount in line 40 less the rate base balance on line 22.  
19

4

5

6

7

12

13

14

15 
program costs over ten years is included in the direct testimony of the 
16 
Company’s Customer Service Panel.  The Company proposes an amortization 
17 
period of five years for the storm deferral, and a twelve-year amortization 
18 
period for legacy meters.   
19

20

F. 
Income Taxes 
1 
Q. 
Please describe how the calculations of State and federal income tax expenses 
2 
were performed. 
3 
A. 
The computation of State income tax is shown on Schedule 9 of the AP-3 
4 
Exhibits.  Starting with operating income before income taxes, we then show 
5 
the various tax adjustments required to determine taxable income, which we 
6 
multiply by the statutory rate of 6.5% to determine the State income tax.  We 
7 
note the calculations exclude the Metropolitan Transportation Authority 
8 
(“MTA”) surcharge rate of 1.91%, which is recovered as part of the current 
9 
MTA surcharge mechanism.   
10 
The computation of federal income tax is shown on Schedule 10 of the AP-3 
11 
Exhibits.  Starting with operating income before income taxes, we then show 
12 
the various tax adjustments required to determine federal taxable income, 
13 
which we multiply by the statutory rate of 21% to determine the current federal 
14 
income tax.  We then show the calculation of deferred federal income tax and 
15 
the amortization of the deferred excess federal income tax to arrive at the total 
16 
federal income tax expense.   
17

A. 
O&M Savings 
20 
Q.        What steps has the Company taken to find O&M savings and mitigate 
21 
customer costs? 
22 
A.        The Company continuously strives to balance the objectives of increasing 
23 


--- Page 42 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
40 
 
productivity and reducing costs while providing safe and reliable service to its 
1 
electric and gas customers.  In late 2017, the Company implemented the BCO 
2 
program to improve processes, functions, and tasks in order to identify savings 
3 
for the Company.  The Company is now implementing initiatives to realize 
4 
those identified savings and, in do so, is transitioning from focusing on an 
5 
independent BCO program to integrating optimization approaches developed 
6 
under BCO to normal business planning and operation. 
7 
Q. 
How have forecasted BCO savings mitigated the proposed revenue 
8 
requirements for RY1, RY2, and RY3? 
9 
A. 
BCO savings projections are based on aggressive savings targets that are 
10 
deliberately challenging for the Company to meet.  In order to mitigate 
11 
customer bills, however, the Company is decreasing its revenue requirements 
12 
by its projections of BCO savings.  This proposal is designed to shield 
13 
customers from the impacts of that risk by passing on the aggressive savings 
14 
projections, even if those savings are not actually achieved. 
15 
The Company includes $3.0 million in savings in RY1, an additional $1.2 
16 
million in savings in RY2 and an additional $1.9 million in RY3 savings for 
17 
electric and $1.4 million in savings in RY1, an additional $0.6 million in 
18 
savings in RY2 and an additional $0.9 million in savings in RY3 for gas, net of 
19 
the costs to achieve.  These BCO savings are reflected in the Company’s 
20 
electric and gas O&M program changes.   
21 
Q. 
The Company also had BCO targets established within the revenue 
22 
requirement in Case 18-E-0067 and 18-G-0068.  How are those targets 
23 


--- Page 43 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
41 
 
incorporated into the revenue requirement in this filing? 
1 
A. 
In 2018, BCO was a nascent program and the Company did not yet have 
2 
reliable savings projections.  The BCO imputations in the current rate plan 
3 
were negotiated to capture for customers a reasonable share of potential 
4 
savings.  In this filing, the Company has provided customers with the higher of 
5 
either its realized savings or the current rate plan imputation for each year.    
6 
For the period from January 2019 through September 2020, realized BCO 
7 
savings reflected in the Historic Year expense levels were higher than those 
8 
imputed.   This reflects that cost-saving process improvements were easier to 
9 
achieve in the initial years of the BCO program.  Specifically, BCO savings 
10 
through September 2020 total $6.7 million, including $3.9 million in savings 
11 
during the Historic Year, compared to a total of $3.1 million in imputed 
12 
savings during 2019 and 2020.  Additionally, in developing the Company’s 
13 
RY1 O&M projection, the Company is incorporating the RY3 imputed 
14 
amounts from the Company’s current rate plan to account for savings during 
15 
the linking period.  These imputed amounts totaling $2.5 million are higher 
16 
than the Company’s developed savings projections for this period.  
17 
Q.   
Is the Company including a one percent labor productivity adjustment its 
18 
O&M projections in addition to the BCO savings? 
19 
A.   
No.  We are including savings that are much greater than the traditional one 
20 
percent labor productivity adjustment.  The BCO savings in this rate filing will 
21 
be over 3.5 times the savings level traditionally included.  In developing its 
22 
labor escalation rates in past base rate cases, the Company has included a one 
23 


--- Page 44 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
42 
 
percent productivity adjustment as a proxy for future potential efficiencies.  In 
1 
the instant proceedings, the Company has performed a thorough study and is 
2 
imputing quantified cost savings.  Instead of waiting to realize concrete 
3 
savings from that program to credit customers, the Company is incorporating 
4 
the projected savings into its proposal in this filing. 
5

B. 
Development of O&M  
7 
Q. 
How were O&M costs developed for the Rate Year? 
8 
A. 
The Company began with Historic Year O&M costs.  Next, the Company 
9 
made adjustments to bring the costs forward to the Rate Year.  Adjustments 
10 
made to expense levels were due to general escalation, labor escalation, 
11

12 
adjustment is described below. 
13 
1. 
General Escalation  
14 
Q. 
Please describe how you escalated costs due to inflation. 
15 
A. 
The general escalation rate is applied to costs anticipated to increase at the rate 
16 
of inflation as measured by the Gross Domestic Product (“GDP”) price 
17 
deflator.  For certain expenses, the escalation factor is specifically tailored to 
18 
the particular expense item, such as medical insurance costs addressed by the 
19 
Company’s Compensation and Benefits Panel, and is presented as a program 
20 
change.  Additional detail on generally escalated costs is included in Schedule 
21 
14 of the AP-3 exhibits. 
22 
Q. 
Please describe how the general escalation rate was applied in developing 
23 
projected revenue requirements. 
24 


--- Page 45 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
43 
 
A. 
The actual GDP deflator used to escalate various non-labor elements of the 
1 
cost of service was published as of October 2020 by the U.S. Bureau of 
2 
Economic Analysis.  The quarterly forecasts for 2020 and 2021, as well as the 
3 
annual forecasts for 2022 and forward, are from the Blue Chip Economic 
4 
Indicators dated October 2020.  Using these forecasts, the projected cumulative 
5 
effect of inflation from the Historic Year to the Rate Year is 3.49 percent.  The 
6 
projected effect of inflation in Rate Years 2 and 3 is 1.60 percent. 
7 
2. 
Labor Escalation  
8 
Q. 
Please describe the labor cost escalation factors used in your projections. 
9 
A. 
Labor cost escalation factors are applied to labor-related elements of expense.  
10 
Labor escalation is reflected on Exhibit AP-3, Schedule 6.   
11 
With respect to employees of the Company’s bargaining unit, Local 503 of the 
12 
International Brotherhood of Electrical Workers (“Local 503”), labor cost 
13 
escalation was projected based on the terms of the collective bargaining 
14 
agreement in effect.  The current agreement between the Company and Local 
15 
503 is in effect through May 31, 2023.  The agreement provided, among other 
16 
things, for general wage increases of 3% on June 1, 2020, 2021, and 2022.  
17 
Accordingly, the escalation rates used in our labor cost projection calculations, 
18 
and reflecting the normalizing adjustments and program changes we explained 
19 
earlier, for employees paid weekly were 3% annually from the end of the 
20 
Historic Year through the Rate Year. 
21 


--- Page 46 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
44 
 
 
The labor costs for employees paid monthly, including escalation applicable to 
1 
the normalizing adjustments and program changes explained earlier, were 
2 
calculated by applying a salary increase of 3% per year.  
3 
3. 
Normalization  
4 
Q. 
Please describe the normalization of O&M costs for the Rate Year. 
5 
A. 
The Company eliminated from the elements of expense (“EOEs”) those 
6 
amounts that are nonrecurring, out of period, or for which the Company has 
7 
decided to not seek recovery in this proceeding.  The Company also annualized 
8 
amounts that were not fully recognized in the Historic Year in order to develop 
9 
Rate Year costs.  Additional detail on normalized costs is found in Schedules 6 
10 
and 15 of the AP-3 exhibits. 
11 
4.

12 
Q.

14 
changes.  These program changes are driven by the business needs of the 
15 
Company.  Estimated costs associated with these programs and additional 
16 
detail regarding these costs are included in Schedules 6 and 15 of the AP-3 
17 
exhibits.

IX. 
COST ALLOCATIONS 
9 
Q. 
Please describe the cost allocation procedures currently used by Orange and 
10 
Rockland to assign or allocate costs to its utility subsidiaries and between the 
11 
Company’s electric and gas operations.  
12 
A. 
Orange and Rockland’s wholly owned utility subsidiary is RECO, which 
13 
provides electric service in New Jersey.  The Company charges costs that it 
14 
incurs for labor, material and services directly to the responsible utility (i.e., 
15 
Orange and Rockland or RECO) to the extent practically identifiable, through 
16 
the use of time sheet reporting and Company specific account numbers.  In 
17 
those instances where work performed is for the common benefit of both 
18 
utilities, costs are allocated through the use of common expense clearing 
19 
accounts and allocations.    
20 
Q. 
What common expense and common plant allocation factors does the 
21 
Company use in these proceedings? 
22 


--- Page 62 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
60 
 
A. 
The Company reviewed the allocation of common expenses and common plant 
1 
in these proceedings and proposes to continue its allocation factors.  The 
2

1. 
Major Storm Reserve (Electric) 
4 
Q.

5

7

8 
over the linking period. 
9 
Q. 
Does the Company anticipate changes to its base rate allowance level on 
10 
Update? 
11 
A. 
Yes.  Consistent with the August 19, 2020 Notice of Apparent Violations 
12 
Related to Tropical Storm Isaias to the Company in Case 20-E-0586, and as 
13 
discussed in the direct testimony of the EIOP, the Company is currently 
14 
working with Con Edison to add crewing capacity and develop other such 
15 
storm plans required by the Notice.  The Company will quantify the annual 
16 
cost impact of these efforts and include such costs in its Update filing. 
17 
Q. 
Is the Company proposing to change its accounting for storm pre-mobilization 
18 
costs? 
19 
A. 
Yes.  Under the Company’s current electric rate plan, the Company may 
20 
charge to the major storm reserve costs above $100,000 per storm incurred to 
21 
obtain the assistance of contractors and/or utility companies providing mutual 
22 
assistance in reasonable anticipation that a storm will affect its electric 
23 


--- Page 66 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
64 
 
operations to the degree meeting the criteria of a “major storm,” but which 
1 
ultimately does not do so.  For pre-staging and mobilization costs in excess of 
2 
$1.75 million, per event, the Company is allowed to charge 85% of such costs 
3 
to the major storm reserve, and the Company expenses 15% of such costs in 
4 
the year incurred.   
5 
The Company is proposing to eliminate the $1.75 million cap per event.  This 
6 
reflects the State’s increased emphasis that the Company should mobilize early 
7 
if a major storm is anticipated to impact the Company’s service territory.  
8 
Because it will be competing for resources with other utilities in the region 
9 
who are also under increased public and political scrutiny, the Company may 
10 
need to bring in more expensive contractors and/or mutual aid crews from 
11 
farther away in order to mobilize appropriately.  These costs are outside the 
12 
Company’s control and should be fully recoverable.

15

16

17

18 
O&M expense with a downward reconciliation over the terms of the rate plans.  
19

20 
over a ten-year period using the Company’s pre-tax rate of return.  With the 
21 
change in recovery methodology, the Company proposes a change in the 
22 
reconciliation mechanism whereby the Company would apply a downward 
23 


--- Page 67 ---

reconciliation to the Company’s total revenue requirement impact of electric 
1 
and gas EE spending over a three-year period (i.e., 2022-2024).  Further, the 
2 
Company proposes to evaluate the downward reconciliation on a portfolio 
3 
inclusive of both electric and gas so as to facilitate flexibility between the 
4 
electric and gas EE programs.  The reconciliation for this combined program 
5

6

3. 
Property Taxes (Electric and Gas) 
10 
Q. 
You mentioned earlier that the Company proposes to continue a property tax 
11 
reconciliation mechanism.  Is the Company proposing to continue the 
12 
reconciliation mechanism as it is currently designed? 
13 
A. 
No.  The current rate plans do not provide for a full and symmetrical property 
14 
tax reconciliation mechanism.  Full reconciliations were in place in Cases 14-
15 
E-0493 and 14-G-0494 and the Company proposes that those mechanisms be 
16 
re-established.   
17 
Q. 
Why does the Company believe that a full and symmetrical property tax 
18 
reconciliation mechanism is appropriate? 
19 
A. 
In her direct testimony, Company witness Merritt explains at length why 
20 
property taxes are not subject to reasonable estimation.  Absent a full and 
21 
symmetrical reconciliation mechanism, these circumstances result in the 
22 
potential for a significant windfall for either customers or the Company at the 
23 


--- Page 68 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
66 
 
expense of the other.  There should be no such opportunity.  The Company 
1 
further notes, as discussed below, that the Commission is particularly inclined 
2 
towards allowing full property tax reconciliations when economic conditions 
3 
are volatile.  The precariousness of the current economic situation is discussed 
4 
at length in the direct testimonies of the Company’s Cost of Capital and Return 
5 
on Equity witnesses. 
6 
 
Moreover, regardless of the process by which the current rate cases are 
7 
concluded (litigated or settled), a large portion of the Company’s property 
8 
taxes for the Rate Year will most likely be unknown in time to be reflected in 
9 
the final revenue requirements.    
10 
Q. 
Should there be a concern that a full and symmetrical property tax mechanism 
11 
will lessen the Company’s incentive to take action to minimize its property tax 
12 
expense? 
13 
A. 
No, not even in the context of a single-year rate plan.  As Company witness 
14 
Merritt explains, the Company has historically sought to minimize its taxes and 
15 
that effort continues on an ongoing basis – it is a normal course of business for 
16 
the Company.  There should be no concern that full reconciliation would 
17 
diminish the Company’s incentive to minimize its property taxes and there is 
18 
no reason to not provide for it because a rate case does not result in a multi-
19 
year rate plan.   
20 
 
The Commission has addressed these concerns in past cases.  For example, in 
21 
Case 08-E-0539, the Commission set rates for Con Edison outside the context 
22 
of a multi-year rate plan and provided for a full and symmetrical reconciliation 
23 


--- Page 69 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
67 
 
of property taxes.  Addressing the disincentive issue on pages 106-107 of its 
1 
April 24, 2009 order in that case, the Commission said: 
2 
 
3 
We share DPS Staff’s concern about removing an incentive for the 
4 
Company to minimize its property tax expenses.  However, the record 
5 
in these cases shows that the Company has aggressively sought to 
6 
minimize its property tax assessments.  Indeed, there is no assertion to 
7 
the contrary.  Moreover, our long standing policy is that a utility will 
8 
be allowed to retain a share of property tax refunds, frequently in the 
9 
10-15% range, to the extent it can be established conclusively that the 
10 
utility’s efforts contributed to that outcome.  Taking these two factors 
11 
into account, we conclude that the Company already has and will 
12 
retain an incentive to minimize its property tax assessments.   
13 
 
14 
Given the magnitude of the Company’s property taxes, the relative 
15 
uncertainty about the impacts of the economic downturn that we 
16 
consider unique, and that the Company will continue to have an 
17 
incentive to minimize its property tax assessments, we are adopting the 
18 
judges’ recommendation for full or bilateral reconciliation of property 
19 
taxes.  (footnotes omitted)  
20 
 
21 
The Commission’s explanation of why a full reconciliation mechanism was 
22 
appropriate in Case 08-E-0539 remains applicable here in the context of a 
23 
single rate year filing.  The Company has continued to aggressively pursue 
24 
minimization of its property taxes.  In addition, the economic circumstances 
25 
the Commission referred to as “unique” in that order are magnified today as 
26 
taxing entities face great fiscal stress and uncertainty due to the ongoing 
27 
COVID-19 pandemic and current economic conditions, which prevents the 
28 
ability to forecast future tax responsibility with any degree of certainty.  
29 
Q. 
What do you propose regarding the sharing between the Company and its 
30 
customers of any property tax savings the Company might obtain? 
31 


--- Page 70 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
68 
 
A. 
The Commission should continue the 86% customer / 14% Company sharing 
1 
mechanism for property tax refunds, including credits against tax payments or 
2 
similar forms of tax reductions (intended to return or offset past overcharges or 
3 
payments determined to have been in excess of the property tax liability 
4 
appropriate for O&R), net of costs incurred to achieve them, that exists under 
5 
the current electric and gas rate plans with one modification.  In many 
6 
instances, the Company is able to negotiate future assessment reductions in a 
7 
property tax settlement, which is more efficient than pursuing lengthy litigation 
8 
in an attempt to obtain a concrete refund award.  The sharing mechanism 
9 
should be modified to include savings from such settlements.  The Company’s 
10 
approach to calculating savings and its underlying rationale for proposing to 
11 
share in such savings is explained by Company witness Merritt.   
12 
This modification to the tax sharing mechanism is consistent with established 
13 
Commission practice to incent utilities to pursue property tax reductions as the 
14 
Commission noted in the 2012 Rate Order (p. 30).  Moreover, as explained by 
15 
Company witness Merritt, the Company’s recent property tax settlements have 
16 
produced material future benefits for customers.

1

2 
1. 
Uncollectible Expense (Electric and Gas) 
3 
Q. 
What is the Company’s proposed accounting treatment for uncollectible 
4 
expenses in this case? 
5 
A. 
The Company proposes a full and symmetrical reconciliation of uncollectible 
6 
expenses. 
7 
Q. 
Why does the Company believe that a full and symmetrical reconciliation is 
8 
warranted? 
9 
A. 
The Company is unable to make an acceptable estimate of uncollectible 
10 
expenses given the continued uncertainty around the financial health of the 
11 
Company’s customers.  The Company continues to see significant growth in its 
12 
aged accounts receivables balances since the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic 
13 
when New York issued its ‘on PAUSE’ and other executive orders and 
14 
economic conditions deteriorated.  Whether those receivables will ultimately 
15 
be collected is dependent on a variety of factors outside of the Company’s 
16 
control.  For instance, the Parker Mosley Act prohibits state utilities, including 
17 
the Company, from disconnecting residential customers during the COVID-19 
18 
state of emergency.  Such prohibition will apply for an additional 180 days 
19 
after the state of emergency ends for residential customers who have 
20 
experienced a change in financial circumstances as a result of the pandemic.  
21 
Although the law is currently set to expire in March 2021, we anticipate the 
22 
law will be extended.  The ultimate impact of this law, as well as the nature of 
23 


--- Page 74 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
72 
 
any other state or federal financial assistance packages and the overall strength 
1 
of the economic recovery, are all variables outside of the Company’s control 
2 
that will dictate the Company’s ability to collect on its receivables. 
3 
Q. 
How does the Company propose to perform the reconciliation calculation? 
4 
A. 
The Company proposes a methodology consistent with how it established the 
5 
uncollectible deferral balance in this case.  The Company first establishes the 
6 
total uncollectible expenses in rates, which consists of the uncollectible 
7 
expense within the revenue requirement plus collections for uncollectibles 
8 
associated with commodity surcharges.  That total is compared to the sum of 
9 
actual uncollectible write-offs plus or minus the change in the uncollectible 
10 
reserve to arrive at the amount to be deferred.  
11 
 
12 
2.

13

14

the carrying charge on the net plant of any displaced project is higher than the

The costs incurred by the Company for implementation of NPSs during the Rate

3. 
Anticipated Laws and Regulations (Electric and Gas) 
8 
Q.

9

10 
A.

11

12 
Community Protection Act (“CLCPA”). 
13 
Q. 
Aren’t these circumstances covered by the “new laws and regulations” 
14

15 
A. 
Yes.  However, application of the new laws provision would subject these 
16 
expenditures to a dollar threshold.  While a dollar threshold has been applied 
17 
for unanticipated costs resulting from a change in law or regulations not 
18 
anticipated at the time rates are set, a threshold should not apply when the 
19 
potential circumstance is known at the time rates are set, although the details of 
20 
implementation are not. 
21 
Q. 
Is there precedent for the Commission permitting deferral of incremental costs 
22 
incurred as a result of anticipated laws? 
23 


--- Page 76 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
74 
 
A, 
Yes.  In various O&R and Con Edison rate cases (e.g., Cases 13-E-0030, et al., 
1 
16-E-0060, et al., 18-E-0067, et al., and 19-E-0065, et al.), the Commission 
2 
has adopted provisions for full deferral of costs associated with specific 
3 
anticipated changes in law.   
4 
Q. 
Why is the Company proposing recovery for additional costs that are expected 
5 
to be incurred to implement new regulations developed pursuant to the 
6 
CLCPA? 
7 
A. 
As discussed in the EIOP testimony, regulations implementing CLCPA are 
8 
still under consideration.  Although the Company anticipates compliance costs 
9 
will be significant, the Company does not know the timing of when it will need 
10 
to be in compliance with the regulations or the full scope of work that the 
11 
Company will need to undertake to comply with the regulations.  As such, the 
12 
Company has not included any projected compliance costs for the anticipated 
13 
regulations in this filing (although it has included projected costs for multi-
14 
value transmission and distribution projects that will further CLCPA goals).    
15 
 
Given that the new regulations are anticipated and compliance costs are 
16 
expected to be substantial, to the extent that the Commission does not 
17 
otherwise specifically provide for recovery of compliance costs associated with 
18 
CLCPA, the Company proposes to defer all O&M expenses in excess of the 
19 
Company’s current Rate Year projection for costs related to compliance with 
20 
the CLCPA.  Similarly, the Company proposes that if capital expenditures 
21 
resulting from compliance with CLCPA cause the Company to exceed its 
22 


--- Page 77 ---

75 
 
aggregate net plant target, the Company be permitted to defer carrying charges 
1 
on the amount of net plant that exceeds the aggregate net plant target.

the AP-3 exhibits. 
10 
Q. 
What is the basis of the financial information presented in the AP-3 Exhibits? 
11 
A. 
Various Company witnesses have presented forecasts extending beyond the 
12 
Rate Year.  There are also proposals by various witnesses, including the 
13 
Accounting Panel, which would affect periods beyond the Rate Year such as 
14 
amortization periods for deferred costs and credits.   
15 
Q. 
Is the Company proposing a multi-year rate plan for adoption by the 
16 
Commission? 
17 
A. 
No.  This filing seeks Commission approval of what is commonly referred to 
18 
as one-year rates.  The Company is, however, interested in pursuing, through 
19 
settlement discussions with Staff and the parties, a multi-year rate plan for each 
20 
gas and electric.  The financial information presented, along with the 
21 
Company’s thoughts on some possible features of multi-year plans, could form 
22 
a basis for discussions to address the myriad of details and complexities that 
23 


--- Page 78 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
76 
 
must be addressed to establish multi-year rate plans that fairly considers the 
1 
interests of all stakeholders.  
2 
 
The Company believes that there is considerable merit to exploring a 
3 
mechanism that would enable the rate plans to be extended beyond the initial 
4 
multi-year terms if certain agreed-upon circumstances exist.  This would go 
5 
beyond the “continuation provision” commonly included in multi-year rate 
6 
plans.  It could reach to automatic modifications of the rate plans that become 
7 
effective at the end of the stated multi-year term.  Examples of the type of 
8 
mechanism would be a tracking mechanism for increasing plant investment or 
9 
the effects of inflation.  The rate plans might also provide for changes in the 
10 
level of recovery of net regulatory assets. 
11 
XII. 
CAPITAL STRUCTURE AND COST OF CAPITAL 
12 
A. 
Fund Requirements and Sources  
13 
Q. 
Are the Company’s projected sources and applications of funds presented in 
14 
the Company’s filing? 
15 
A. 
Yes.  Schedule 12 of the AP-3 Exhibits, presents a statement of sources and 
16 
application of funds for the Rate Year for electric and gas operations.  Sources 
17 
of funds are separated into internal and external sources.  Internal sources 
18 
would generally include the change in retained earnings during the Rate Year, 
19 
depreciation, amortizations and deferred taxes.  External sources would 
20 
generally include long-term debt and common stock equity.   The primary use 
21 
of funds would generally be for construction and the retirement of debt.  These 
22 
exhibits identify those projected for the Rate Year. 
23 


--- Page 79 ---
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.  
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
ACCOUNTING PANEL 
 
77 
 
B. 
Financial Ratios 
1 
Q. 
Please describe Schedule 13 of the AP-3 Exhibits. 
2 
A. 
Schedule 13 of those exhibits presents the interest coverage ratios for Orange 
3 
and Rockland for the Historic Year and the last three available calendar years.    
4 
C. 
Rate of Return  
5 
Q. 
Is the Accounting Panel sponsoring an exhibit regarding the Company’s 
6 
required rate of return? 
7 
A. 
Yes, along with Company witness Saegusa, we are sponsoring Exhibit AP-5, 
8 
which provides detail on the Company’s proposed rate of return.  The inputs to 
9 
these exhibits are discussed in detail in witness Saegusa’s testimony.  
10 
Q. 
Will the rate of return be updated in this proceeding? 
11 
A. 
The Company may update the rate of return as part of the Company’s rebuttal 
12 
and update testimony if financial conditions at that time warrant such an 
13 
update. 
14 
Q. 
Does that conclude your pre-filed direct testimony? 
15 
A.  
Yes, it does.

Facilities Management 
 2021  
1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Blooming Grove BMS System 
Project/Program Manager: Kevin Duggan 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 21485565 
Status:  ☐ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☒ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 2021 
Estimated Date In Service: 2022 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $500 
O&M:  
 
B.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M:

C. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
The BG Building Management System (BMS) is a project to upgrade the heating, 
ventilation and air conditioning (HVAC) controls throughout the Company’s 
Blooming Grove operating center. The existing controls are pneumatic and the system 
is old and leaking control air, making it difficult to operate the system properly.  The 
BG BMS System will include electronic controls on all the equipment with a software 
package that allows for remote oversight. 
 
 
Justification Summary: 
The current system is outdated and past its functional life. The system has to be 
replaced in order to maintain the Blooming Grove operating center with proper 
conditioning. The ventilation system is critical at this time to provide proper air 
exchanges to remove air contaminated with Carbon Dioxide (CO2) and Covid-19. 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
This project aligns with the Company’s long-range plans to minimize our facility’s 
carbon footprint, as the new system will be more energy efficient. 
 
 
 
 
 


--- Page 149 ---
Exhibit AP___6 
Schedule 1 
Page 2 of 4 
 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
There is no alternative as the system is at the end of its life. A new system is critical to 
maintaining the building 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
This alternative is not recommended as many groups would have to purchase 
portable equipment and there would always be an issue with connectivity. 
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
 
 
 
Alternative 3 description and reason for rejection 
 
 
 
Risk of No Action 
Give the consequences, including enterprise risks that might arise by not doing the 
project/ program. Quantify the risks, if applicable. 
 
Risk 1  No action will leave the space with no technology upgrades and require 
personnel to determine if the space works for their particular meeting. Many groups 
would need to look elsewhere for meeting space including off site. 
 
 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
• Increased energy efficiency and employee safety/comfort

4. Definitions 
 
Total Funding Request: All funding requested for program or project over program/project lifecycle or 
for on-going programs the five-year requested amount, including all capital, O&M, retirement. 
 
Cost Savings: Reductions in costs that are currently being incurred (e.g., reduced annual maintenance 
cost relative to today) 
 
Cost Avoidance: Reductions in anticipated future costs that don’t occur today (e.g., anticipated short-
term fixes/maintenance if capital isn’t deployed) 
 
Project Status: 
 
• 
Initiation – New project, not authorized yet 
• 
Planning – Project authorized, not started yet 
• 
Executing – Project in-flight  
• 
On-going – Annual program

This project is to remove and replace the asbestos clad windows at the Company’s 
Port Jervis office (16 Pike Street, Port Jervis, NY) with new energy efficient windows, 
as well upgrade the exterior siding on the building. 
 
This building was left vacant in 2012 when the operations group was shifted to 
Westfall, PA.  Due to the sale of Pike County Light & Power Company in 2018, 
electric operations staff returned to this building.  Minimal maintenance had been 
performed on the building in anticipation of selling the property. 
 
The windows have asbestos caulk and are single pane glass.  The entire storefront 
including 32 windows and 1 door entranceway needs to be replaced as the aluminum 
frames are degrading. The brick work also needs attention. 
 
Justification Summary: 
The building needs to be maintained now that operations staff has relocated to it.  If a 
window breaks, it will take an asbestos contractor to remove and replace the window.  
Energy is lost due to the single pane glass. The building is one of the first things seen 
by travelers as they enter NY from PA and it needs to be brought up to the 
Company’s standards.

Work Description: 
• Real Estate Optimization is a project directed by Con Edison’s Real Estate 
department to investigate ways to reduce both Con Edison’s and O&R’s 
operating costs by reducing their rental footprint. 
• Real Estate, in cooperation with O&R’s Facilities department, is developing a 
plan to relocate office staff located in rental properties, such as One Blue Hill 
Plaza in Pearl River, NY, into existing office space currently owned by O&R.  
Renovations to the owned office space will be required to facilitate the 
relocations. 
• Expenditures expected will be for Architectural/Engineering planning, design 
and construction oversight, as well as the actual construction costs to renovate 
the O&R owned spaces to facilitate staff relocation from the leased spaces.   
 
 
 
Justification Summary: 
The Company continues to investigate ways to reduce its operating expenses.  The 
Company has implemented a program, Business Cost Optimization (BCO), to 
mitigate operation and maintenance related cost increases. As part of this BCO effort, 
the Real Estate department began a program to reduce expenses by reducing the 
leases for both office space and land that is no longer needed by the Company.

Project/Program Title: Spring Valley Fuel Station Canopy 
Project/Program Manager: Kevin Duggan 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 22652805 
Status:  ☐ Initiation  ☒ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________

Work Description: 
 
• 
This project consists of the design and installation of a canopy over the fuel station 
islands at the Spring Valley Operating Center. 
• 
Work includes hiring an Engineering firm to design the canopy which will span two 
fuel station islands that hold dispensing stations for gasoline, and diesel.  The canopy 
also will cover a special station to fill fuel tankers operated by the Company’s 
transportation group that are used to provide fuel to equipment at remote locations. 
• 
This project may require approval from the Town of Ramapo Planning Board which 
would require the Company to retain outside legal counsel to assist in the application 
and approval process. 
 
Construction of the canopy will be competitively bid and constructed with oversight 
by trained Company staff 
Justification Summary: 
The Company installed the existing fuel station equipment in 2013.  The equipment has been 
exposed to the weather and is showing signs of deterioration. Installation of a canopy has 
been shown to reduce the deterioration of the exposed equipment. The Company has 
installed canopies at its Blooming Grove and Middletown Operating Centers.  As a result, the 
equipment at those two locations is protected and in good condition. 
 
In addition, Company personnel using the station are subject to inclement weather and 
potential injury when snow/ice builds up around the dispensers.

Alternatives

be diminished. 
 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 – If no action is taken, the fuel station equipment will continue to degrade at an 
accelerated rate than if no canopy were in place.  In addition, the safety concerns that 
arise while fueling vehicles in inclement weather will persist. 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
• Increased safety and more reliable fueling equipment

1. Project / Program Summary
Type:  ☐ Project  ☒ Program 
Category:  ☐ Capital  ☒ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title:  Emergency Preparedness Support Services - Specialist/Senior Specialist 
Project/Program Manager: Don Higgins 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 
Status:  ☐ Initiation  ☒ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 1/1/22 
Estimated Date In Service: 12/31/22 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)
Capital: 
O&M: $463.6 
B. 
☐5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000)
☐5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000)
O&M: 
Capital: 
C.
5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000)
O&M: 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:
(Years/months) (If applicable)
Work Description: 
This new position in the Emergency Preparedness (“EP”) organization will assist the existing Senior 
Specialist position in the execution of all tasks associated with gas emergency planning, preparedness, 
and response for the Company. This position will support continuity of operations both on “blue sky 
days” as well as during event response and restoration. The position will ultimately support the 
Company’s identified Enterprise Risk Management findings (“ERMs”) and the three strategic 
priorities: 1) Safety, 2) Operational Excellence, and 3) Enhancing the Customer Experience.  
Justification Summary: 
Several of Orange and Rockland Corporate Risks have the potential to significantly impact the gas 
delivery system. These risks could impact the entire Company, as well as external stakeholders.   
Without proper preparedness measures and resources, the Company’s response and reputation are 
undermined. Any event with the potential to impact the gas delivery system can result in widespread 
service interruptions and challenge existing resources. Additionally, an increased focus on the Gas 
business and the implications of less than adequate preparedness in that area, has made risk mitigation 
imperative for this line of business. With the additional position, existing mitigation, preparedness, 
and response programs and practices can be enhanced and more robust ones developed.   
Risk mitigation strategies include EPs projects, programs, and resources (internal and external) to 
address risk. It is important to engage the Company’s workforce and prepare it to successfully execute 
their Emergency Response Plan (“ERP”) and other guiding documents. The value of an additional EP 
representative comes from the employee’s ability to efficiently and effectively collaborate with and 
support Gas operations in at least five core areas: 1) risk mitigation planning, implementation, and 
progress monitoring (e.g., benchmarking; Gas ERP); 2) instilling and training on core Incident 
Command System (“ICS”) principles; 3) compliance and communication with external agencies, 
Exhibit AP-6 
Schedule 5 
Page 1 of 7


--- Page 165 ---
2 
 
regulators and other stakeholders; 4) assisting in developing an exercise and training program; and 5) 
Co-facilitator of the after action process following exercises and incidents.  
 
Emergency Preparedness strives to effectively manage the Company’s emergency preparedness, 
response, and recovery efforts. The increasing needs and expectations of customers (both internal and 
external), regulators, and agency partners, as well as increased regulatory oversight, as it pertains to 
Gas safety and awareness/education , presents, and will continue to present, a challenge with limited 
staffing. 
 
Emergency Preparedness’ efforts and programs support all organizations/commodities within the 
Company as it relates to emergency preparedness, response, and recovery efforts. As an example, the 
commitment to increase the number of Gas related exercises and training opportunities conducted on 
an annual basis with first responders.  Further, the company will work with Staff, local fire 
departments, and emergency management organizations to adopt the principles of the Pipeline 
Emergency Responders Initiative (“PERI”).  
 
Emergency Preparedness leads the design and execution of a tabletop and a functional exercise that 
encompasses the Gas group and electric as well as, agency first responders (Police/Fire/Office of 
Emergency Management). This exercise, and the annual Operations Exercise, are large and complex 
undertakings, and require the assistance of a vendor because of current limited staffing. Emergency 
Preparedness also assists with the designing and facilitation of the Orange and Rockland 101 Seminar. 
In this course, Emergency Preparedness staff will outline the coordination between utility and first 
responders during the initial response to an event. This course is offered to all first response agencies, 
municipalities and other stakeholders in the Orange and Rockland service territory in regional 
offerings at local training centers (e.g., Rockland County Fire Training Center, Orange County 
Emergency Operations Center).   
 
Currently, Emergency Preparedness has only a single employee conducting external agency training 
and support. This employee spends approximately 120 – 220 hours, annually, conducting these 
activities. This relationship, and the service provided, is one of the most valued services provided by 
the Company, as evidenced by feedback from external responder agencies.   
 
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
Large Scale Gas Customer Outage risk was added to the Con Edison Inc. risk profile in the fourth 
quarter of 2019 and then to Orange and Rockland risk profile in the second quarter of 2020. A 
significant driver for adding this risk stemmed from two recent major industry events which resulted 
in large scale gas customer outages (the 2018 Columbia Gas Event in Massachusetts, and 
the 2019 National Grid Aquidneck Island Outage in Rhode Island), where over 7,000 gas customers lost 
service in each event. As a result, it was recognized that gas utilities, including Orange and Rockland, 
need to improve the capability respond to a similar large event, especially if it occurs in the winter or 
lasts for an extended period of time (e.g., multiple weeks).  
 
Some of the existing major risk mitigation efforts include: 
 
• 
Corporate Response to Incidents and Emergencies Guideline  
• 
Gas Emergency Response Plan 
• 
Risk Event Guides 
• 
Exercise and Training Programs 
• 
Monthly Officers and Chiefs Meetings 
Exhibit AP-6 
Schedule 5 
Page 2 of 7


--- Page 166 ---
3 
 
• 
Mutual Assistance Agreements 
• 
External Agency and Regulatory Relationships 
• 
Weather and Security Risk Assessment 
• 
Benchmarking 
• 
Systems and Applications (Gas Outage Management System -future)  
 
 
Maintaining and enhancing these mitigation efforts safeguard the Company’s energy delivery systems 
and facilities, response, and reputation. These mitigation efforts also contribute to the success of 
operational mitigation efforts, such as improved emergency response and restoration, efficient and 
accurate communication with regulatory agencies and other external stakeholders, and reduced cost to 
customers.   
 
 
 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
Con Edison EP personnel have been used on an as-needed basis to assist with the increased workload, 
especially during weather events and field emergencies. However, availability of this resource is 
contingent upon the needs of Con Edison EP being met by other department personnel. Historically, 
Orange and Rockland’s support needs occur simultaneously with Con Edison’s, reducing the 
practicality of this alternative. Given the high level of specialization associated with the execution of 
the Company’s emergency management philosophy and processes, there are no other viable 
alternatives under consideration. 
 
 
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
 
 
 
Alternative 3 description and reason for rejection 
 
 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
Given the significant number of recommendations and anticipated revisions made to the Emergency 
Response Plan, along with the additional workload associated with these initiatives, and a continued 
focus on the need to enhance gas emergency preparedness, the process may not be performed at the 
historically high levels consistent with the expectations of the department from its constituents. 
Without additional staffing, the Emergency Preparedness group will be challenged to maintain, and, 
more importantly, enhance the identified risk mitigation efforts for Gas Operations.   
Exhibit AP-6 
Schedule 5 
Page 3 of 7


--- Page 167 ---
4 
 
 
 
 
Risk 2 
 
 
 
Risk 3 
 
 
 
 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
• 
Better Use of Staff During Events -- Because of increased work volume during storms and other 
major events, the Section Manager has historically split his/her efforts between developing 
strategic objectives, and the tactical response laid out for the Sr. Specialist to achieve those 
objectives. The tactical response has been concentrated on sharing the tasks and duties of the Sr. 
Specialist along geographical boundaries. Having an additional position will allow the Section 
Manager to focus more effort and energy on the strategic objectives and responding to high-level 
inquiries. 
• 
24 Hr./Day Coverage During Events -- EP presently uses a split-shift schedule to provide 18 
hr./day response coverage during major events. An additional position will allow for full 24 
hr./day coverage which is typically needed. 
• 
Improved Communications with Stakeholders -- The addition of a position will help to alleviate the 
bottleneck created as a result of availability of only one Sr. Specialist, and a manager, during major 
events and would also give an opportunity to have one person solely dedicated to providing 
strategic support to the Gas group. 
• 
Increased Training & Exercising -- Having an additional position will allow for an enhanced 
training and exercise program inclusive of refresher training and Incident Command System 
training for both internal and external stakeholders.   
• 
Day-To-Day Operations -- To better serve the Company’s EP administrative needs, the additional 
position will allow for sharing the duties of the position with the incumbent Sr. Specialist.

Cost estimate is based on the wages and benefits for other similar existing specialists within the 
department. 
 
 
5. Conclusion 
In order to provide consistent and robust service, additional staff is required. The administrative and 
non-administrative duties associated with maintaining a robust and effective Emergency Preparedness 
program are currently performed by one Senior Specialist under the direction of a Section Manager 
(whose time is split -- 40% supporting Orange and Rockland and 60% supporting Con Edison for all 
commodities). An additional staff member would ease the administrative burden and allow the 
department to focus on more critical strategic objectives and programs with the Gas Department.   
 
This additional resource will enhance and increase the number of current Exercise Programs for Gas, 
so that greater proficiency is achieved (e.g., work with all departments towards more effective 
exercises, and a building-block approach towards the development of annual Gas exercises). This 
person will work with Gas Operations and other support groups to enhance O&R’s risk assessment 
resources (e.g., storm hardening, changing weather patterns, Cyber-attacks; rogue employee 
involvement, and possibly a new OMS which may require a re-calibration of our matrices), and 
introduce/utilize new systems and applications in order to better execute the ERP (e.g., automated 
call-out system, crew management, OMS etc.). Additionally, this resource will enhance our external 
agency training and relationships (e.g. NY-Pipeline Emergency Responders Initiative participation; 
Gas safety training; exercises; electric safety storm training,) along with providing additional coverage 
during major events.

4. Definitions 
 
Total Funding Request: All funding requested for program or project over program/project lifecycle or 
for on-going programs the five-year requested amount, including all capital, O&M, retirement. 
 
Cost Savings: Reductions in costs that are currently being incurred (e.g., reduced annual maintenance 
cost relative to today) 
Exhibit AP-6 
Schedule 5 
Page 6 of 7


--- Page 170 ---
7 
 
 
Cost Avoidance: Reductions in anticipated future costs that don’t occur today (e.g., anticipated short-
term fixes/maintenance if capital isn’t deployed) 
 
Project Status: 
 
• 
Initiation – New project, not authorized yet 
• 
Planning – Project authorized, not started yet 
• 
Executing – Project in-flight  
• 
On-going – Annual program
